8.19447pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Mask}}}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}_{F}}}}\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F-L}}\hskip 7.3612pt}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;M:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Mask}}}$}}}\par\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle\frac{H=\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i}\quad\quad\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}_{F}}}}\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+F}}\quad\Gamma,x:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}}}A\vdash N:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}\quad\quad{D=\{\ell_{i}:A_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}\}_{i}}\quad[\Gamma,p_{i}:A_{i},{r_{i}}:B_{i}\to B\vdash N_{i}:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}]_{i}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Handler}}}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\frac{H=\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}_{F}}}}\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+F}}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,x:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}}}A\vdash N:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt{D=\{\ell_{i}:A_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}\}_{i}}\hskip 8.19447pt[\Gamma,p_{i}:A_{i},{r_{i}}:B_{i}\to B\vdash N_{i}:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}]_{i}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Handler}}}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle\frac{H=\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i}\hskip 7.3612pt\hskip 7.3612pt\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}_{F}}}}\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+F}}\hskip 7.3612pt\Gamma,x:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}}}A\vdash N:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}\hskip 7.3612pt\hskip 7.3612pt{D=\{\ell_{i}:A_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}\}_{i}}\hskip 7.3612pt[\Gamma,p_{i}:A_{i},{r_{i}}:B_{i}\to B\vdash N_{i}:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}]_{i}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Handler}}}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{H=\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i}\hskip 7.3612pt\hskip 7.3612pt\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}_{F}}}}\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+F}}\hskip 7.3612pt\Gamma,x:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}}}A\vdash N:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}\hskip 7.3612pt\hskip 7.3612pt{D=\{\ell_{i}:A_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}\}_{i}}\hskip 7.3612pt[\Gamma,p_{i}:A_{i},{r_{i}}:B_{i}\to B\vdash N_{i}:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}]_{i}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Handler}}}$}}}

Figure 2
.
Typing rules for
Met
.

Modality Introduction and Elimination

Modalities are introduced by
T-Mod
and eliminated by

T-Letmod
.
The term
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,V
introduces modality
\mu
to the type of the
conclusion and lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}
into the context of the
premise, and requires the value
V
to be well-typed under the new
effect context
E
manipulated by
\mu
.
The lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}
tracks the change to the effect context.
Specialising the modality
\mu
to either absolute or relative
modalities, we get the following two rules.

\frac{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{[E]}_{F}}}}\vdash V:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[E]}\,V:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\par\frac{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}_{F}}}}\vdash V:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+(F-L)}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}\,V:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}

Note that we use modality
\mu
instead of concrete modality
\mu_{F}
in
types and terms, because the index can always be inferred from the
effect context.
We restrict
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}
to values to avoid effect leakage
(
Lorenzen et al., 2024
;
Ahman, 2023
)
.
Otherwise, a term such as
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\ell:P\rangle}}\,({{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\,V)
would type
check under the empty effect context but get stuck due to the unhandled
operation
\ell
.

The term
\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M
moves the modality
\mu
from
the type of
V
to the binding of
x
.
As with boxing, unboxing is restricted to values.
Following MTT, we use let-style modality elimination
which takes another modality
\nu
in addition to the modality
\mu

that is eliminated from
V
.
This is crucial for sequential unboxing.
For instance, the following term sequentially unboxes
x:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A
. The variables
y
and
z
are bound as

y\@ifmtarg{\nu}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A
and
z\@ifmtarg{\nu\circ\mu}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu\circ\mu}}}}A
,
respectively.

\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\nu}\;y=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;z=y\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M

Masking and Handling

Masking and handling also introduce relative modalities.
Unlike
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}
, these constructs can apply to computations as they
perform masking and handling semantically.
In
T-Mask
, the mask
{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;M
removes effects
L
from the
ambient effect context for
M
.
For the return value of
M
, we need to box it with
{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}
to
reconcile the mismatch between
F-L
and
F
.
In
T-Handler
, the handler
{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H
extends the ambient
effect context with effects
D
for
M
.
For the return value of
M
which is bound as
x
in the return
clause, we need to box it with
{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}
to reconcile the mismatch
between
D+F
and
F
.
The other parts of the handler rule are standard.

Accessing Variables

The
T-Var
rule uses the auxiliary judgement

\Gamma\vdash(\mu,A)\Rightarrow\nu\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}
defined in

Figure

1
.
Variables of absolute types can always be used as they do not depend
on the effect context.
For a non-absolute term variable binding
x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}}A
from
context
\Gamma,x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}}A,\Gamma^{\prime}
, we must guarantee that it
is safe to use
x
in the current effect context.
The term bound to
x
is defined inside
\mu
under the effect context

F
.
As we track all transformations on effect contexts up to the binding
of
x
as locks in
\Gamma^{\prime}
, the current effect context
E
is
obtained by applying
\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})
to
F
.
Thus, we need the transformation
\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})_{F}
to hold
for effect safety.

Subeffecting

Subeffecting is incorporated into the
T-Var
rule within the
transformation relation
\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\nu_{F}
.
We have seen how subeffecting works in

Section

2.4
.
We give another example here which upcasts the empty effect context to

E
.
It is well-typed because
[]_{\cdot}\Rightarrow[E]_{\cdot}
holds.

\lambda x^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{Int}})}.\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\;y=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[E]}\,y:{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{Int}})}\to{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]}}}({\texttt{Int}}\to{\texttt{Int}})}

4.6.
Operational Semantics

The operational semantics for
Met
is quite
standard
(
Hillerström et al., 2020
)
.
We first define evaluation contexts
\mathcal{E}
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Evaluation contexts}&\mathcal{E}&::=&[~]\mid\mathcal{E}\;N\mid V\;\mathcal{E}\mid{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;\mathcal{E}\mid{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;\mathcal{E}\mid{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H\\
\end{array}

The reduction rules are as follows.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}@{}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\text{{E-App}}&(\lambda x^{A}.M)\,V&\leadsto&M[V/x]\\
\text{{E-Letmod}}&\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x={{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M&\leadsto&M[V/x]\\
\text{{E-Mask}}&{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\,V&\leadsto&{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}}\,V\\
\text{{E-Ret}}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;V\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}\,V)/x],\hfill\text{where }({{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Op}}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;V]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[V/p,(\lambda y.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}[y]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H)/r],\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{ where }0{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})\text{ and }(\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Lift}}&\mathcal{E}[M]&\leadsto&\mathcal{E}[N],\hfill\text{if }M\leadsto N\\
\end{array}

The only slightly non-standard aspect of the rules is the boxing of
values escaping masks and handlers in
E-Mask
and
E-Ret
.
They coincide with the typing rules for masks and handlers.
In
E-Ret
, we assume handlers are decorated with the operations

D
that they handle as in
Section

2
.

Following
Biernacki et al. (2018)
, the predicate
n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})

is defined inductively on evaluation contexts as follows.
We have
n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})
if there are
n
masks for

\ell
in
\mathcal{E}
without corresponding handlers.
The
E-Op
rule requires
0{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})
to guarantee that
the current handler is not masked by masks in
\mathcal{E}
.
The meta function
\mathsf{count}(\ell;L)
yields the number of
\ell

labels in
L
.
We omit the inductive cases that do not change
n
.

\frac{}{0{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,[~])}\par\frac{n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})}{n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell^{\prime}\,\mathcal{E})}\par\frac{n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})\quad\mathsf{count}(\ell;L)=m}{(n+m){-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\,\mathcal{E})}\\
\par\frac{(n+1){-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})\quad\ell\in\mathsf{dom}(H)}{n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H)}\par\frac{n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})\quad\ell\notin\mathsf{dom}(H)}{n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H)}

4.7.
Type Soundness and Effect Safety

We prove type soundness and effect safety for
Met
. Our proofs cover
the extensions in
Section

6
.

Met
enjoys substitution properties along the lines of

Kavvos and Gratzer (2023)
.
For example, we have the following rule for substituting values with
modalities into terms.

\frac{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}\vdash V:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F^{\prime}}}\quad\Gamma,x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}}A,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad}{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash M[V/x]:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}

We state and prove the relevant properties in
Section

B.3
.

To state syntactic type soundness, we first define normal forms.

Definition 4.2 (Normal Forms).

We say a term
M
is in a normal form with respect to effect type

E
, if it is either
a value
V

or of the form
M=\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;V]
for
\ell\in E
and
n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})
.

The following together give type soundness and effect safety (proofs
in
Sections

B.4
and
B.5
).

Theorem 4.3 (Progress).

If
\,\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
, then either there exists
N
such that

M\leadsto N
or
M
is in a normal form with respect to
E
.

Theorem 4.4 (Subject Reduction).

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
and
M\leadsto N
, then

\Gamma\vdash N:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
.

5.
Encoding Effect Polymorphism in
Met

Even without effect variables,
Met
is sufficiently expressive to
encode programs from conventional row-based effect systems provided
effect variables on function arrows always refer to the lexically
closest one.
This is an important special case, since most functions in practice
use at most one effect variable.
For example, as of July 2024, the
Koka
repository contains 520
effectful functions across 112 files but only 86 functions across 5
files use more than one effect variable, almost all of them internal
primitives for handlers not exposed to programmers.
Moreover, almost all programs in the
Frank
repository make no mention
of effect variables, relying on syntactic sugar to hide the single
effect variable.
We formally characterise and prove this intuition on the
expressiveness of
Met
.

5.1.
Row Effect Types with a Single Effect Variable

We first define
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
, a core calculus with row-based effect types in
the style of Koka
(
Leijen, 2017
)
, but where each scope can only refer to
the lexically closest effect variable.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Types}&A,B&::=&1\mid A\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}B\mid\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon}}.A\\
\textrm{Effects}&L,D,E,F&::=&\cdot\mid\ell,E\\
\textrm{Contexts}&\Gamma&::=&\cdot\mid\Gamma,x:_{\varepsilon}A\mid\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{E}}}\mid\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{E}}}\\
\textrm{Values}&V,W&::=&x\mid\lambda^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}x^{A}.M\mid\Lambda{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon}}.V\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Terms}&M,N&::=&{()}\mid x\mid\lambda^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}x^{A}.M\mid M\,N\\
&&\mid&\Lambda{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon}}.V\mid M\,\#{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}\mid{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;M\\
&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;M\mid{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H\\
\textrm{Handlers}&H&::=&\{\textnormal{{return}}\;x\mapsto M\}\mid\{\ell\,p\,r\mapsto M\}\uplus H\\
\end{array}

In types we include units, effectful functions, and effect abstraction

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon}}.A
. As we consider only one effect
variable at a time, we need not track effect variables on function
types and effect abstraction.
Nonetheless, we include them in grey font for easier comparison with
existing calculi.
In
\Gamma
, each term variable is annotated with the effect variable

\varepsilon
that was referred to at the time of its introduction.
Further, we add markers
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{E}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{E}}}
to
the context, which track the change of effects due to functions,
masks, handlers, and effect abstraction.
These markers are not needed by the typing rules but help with the
encoding. As with
Met
, we require contexts to be ordered.
For simplicity we assume operations are always present and defined by
a global context
\Sigma=\{\overline{\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B}\}
, thus unifying
extensions
D
, masks
L
, and effect contexts
E
of
Met
into one
syntactic category.
Mirroring our kind restriction for operation arrows in
Met
, we
assume that these
A
and
B
are not function arrows, but they can be
effect abstractions (which may themselves contain function arrows).

[formula]

\frac{\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}\text{ or }\quad\quad A=\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}}.A^{\prime}\text{ or }A=1}{\Gamma_{1},x:_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}A,\Gamma_{2}\vdash x:A\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Var}}}\quad\!\frac{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{E}}},x:_{\varepsilon}A\vdash M:B\,!\,{\{{F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}x^{A}.M:A\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}B\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Abs}}}\quad\!\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:A\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}B\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash N:A\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash M\,N:B\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-App}}}\par\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\Gamma)\quad\quad\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{E}}}\vdash V:A\,!\,{\{{\cdot}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon^{\prime}\}}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash\Lambda{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.V:\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.A\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-EAbs}}}\quad\ \ \frac{\Gamma\vdash M:\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.A\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}{\Gamma\vdash M\,\#{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}:A[{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}/]\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-EApp}}}\quad\ \ \frac{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{L+E}}}\vdash M:A\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;M:A\,!\,{\{{L+E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Mask}}}\par\frac{(\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B)\in\Sigma\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash M:A\,!\,{\{{\ell,E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;M:B\,!\,{\{{\ell,E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Do}}}\quad\ \frac{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{E}}}\vdash M:A\,!\,{\{{\overline{\ell_{i}},E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}\quad\Gamma,x:_{\varepsilon}A\vdash N:B\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}\quad\quad\{\ell_{i}:A_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}\}\subseteq\Sigma\quad[\Gamma,p_{i}:_{\varepsilon}A_{i},{r_{i}}:_{\varepsilon}B_{i}\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}B\vdash N_{i}:B\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}]_{i}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i}:B\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Handler}}}

Figure 3
.
Typing rules of
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
.

Figure

3
gives the typing rules of
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
.
The judgement
\Gamma\vdash M:A\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}
states
that in context
\Gamma
, the term
M
has type
A
and might use
concrete effects
E
extended with effect variable
\varepsilon
.
The typing rules are mostly standard for row-based effect systems.
The
R-Var
rule ensures that either the current effect
variable matches the effect variable at which the variable was
introduced or that the value is an effect abstraction or unit.
These constraints guarantee that programs can only refer to one effect
variable in one scope.
The
R-App
,
R-Do
,
R-Mask
, and
R-Handler

rules are standard.
The
R-Abs
rule is standard except for requiring the effect
variable to remain unchanged.
The
R-EAbs
rule introduces a new effect variable
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
the
R-EApp
rule instantiates an effect abstraction.
While conventional systems allow instantiating with any effect row,

R-EApp
only allows instantiation with the ambient effects
E

and effect variable
\varepsilon
.
The instantiation operator
[{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}/]
implements
standard type substitution for the single effect variable as follows.

\begin{array}[]{c}1[{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}/]\,=\,1\qquad\qquad(\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.A)[{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}/]\,=\,\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.A\\
(A\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon^{\prime}\}}}}B)[{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}/]\,=\,A[{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}/]\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F,E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}B[{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}/]\\
\end{array}

For example, the following
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
function (grey parts omitted) sums up
all yielded integers.

{{\begin{array}[]{r@{~~}c@{~~}l}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{asSum}}}}}}}&:&\forall.(1\to^{{{\texttt{Gen Int}}}{}}1)\to{\texttt{Int}}\\
\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{asSum}}}}}}}&=&\Lambda.\lambda f.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;f\;()\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto 0,{\texttt{yield}}\;x\;r\mapsto x+r\;{()}\}\end{array}

5.2.
Encoding

We now give compositional translations for types and contexts of
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
into
Met
.
We transform
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
types at effect context
E
to modal types in

Met
by the translation
\llbracket-\rrbracket_{E}
.

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket 1\rrbracket_{E}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\rangle}}}}1\\
\llbracket A\to^{F}B\rrbracket_{E}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}}}}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket_{F})\\
\llbracket\forall.A\rrbracket_{E}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{E}&=&\cdot\\
\llbracket\Gamma,x:A\rrbracket_{E}&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E},x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{E}}}}}A^{\prime}\text{ for }{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A^{\prime}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}\\
\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{F}}}\rrbracket_{E}&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{F},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{F-E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E-F\rangle}}}}\\
\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{F}}}\rrbracket_{\cdot}&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{F},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\\
\end{array}

For the unit type, we insert the identity modality for the uniformity
of the translation.
For a function arrow
A\to^{F}B
, we use a relative modality

{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}
to wrap the function arrow.
This modality transforms the outside effect context
E
to
F
which
is specified by the original function arrow
A\to^{F}B
.
For effect abstraction, we use an empty absolute modality to wrap the
type, simulating entering a new scope with different effect variables.
We translate contexts by translating each type and moving top-level
modalities to their bindings. For each marker, we insert a corresponding
lock to reflect the changes of effect context.

As an example of type translation, the type of
asSum
in

\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
from
Section

5.1
is translated to

{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{asSum}}}}}}}&:&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}{\texttt{Gen Int}}\rangle}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\rangle}}}}1\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\rangle}}}}1)\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\rangle}}}}{\texttt{Int}}).\\
\end{array}

Observe that not every valid typing judgement in
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
can be
transformed to a valid typing judgement in
Met
, because the translation
depends on markers in contexts, while the typing of
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
does not.
We define well-scoped typing judgements, which characterise the typing
judgements for which our encoding is well-defined, as follows.

Definition 5.1 (Well-scoped).

A typing judgement
\Gamma_{1},x:_{\varepsilon}A,\Gamma_{2}\vdash M:B\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}
is
well-scoped for
x
if either
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(M)
or
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{F}}}\notin\Gamma_{2}
or
A=\forall.A^{\prime}
or
A=1
. A typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash M:A\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}
is
well-scoped
if it is well-scoped for all
x\in\Gamma
.

In particular, if the judgement at the leaf of a derivation tree is
well-scoped, then every judgement in the derivation tree is
well-scoped.
Also note that contexts with no markers are always well-scoped.
Consequently, restricting judgements to well-scoped ones in
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
does
not reduce expressive power, as we can always ensure that contexts at
the leaves have no markers.

We write
M:A\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow M^{\prime}
for the translation of a
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
term
M

of type
A
with effect context
E
to a
Met
term
M^{\prime}
.
We have the following type preservation theorem.
The translation of terms and proof of type preservation can be found
in
Appendix

C
.

Lemma 5.2 (Type preservation of encoding).

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A\,!\,{\{{E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}
is well-scoped, then
M:A\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow M^{\prime}

and
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash M^{\prime}:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
.

Intuitively, the term translation inserts boxing and unboxing
constructs in appropriate places in order to realise transition
between effect contexts as embodied by the type translation.
Specifically, for variables we unbox them immediately after they are
bound, and re-box them when they are used.
As an example of term translation, we can translate the

asSum
handler in
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
defined in
Section

5.1
as
follows into
Met
, omitting boxing and unboxing of the identity
modality
{\langle\rangle}
.

{\begin{array}[]{r@{~~}c@{~~}l}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{asSum}}}}}}}&=&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}(\lambda f.\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}{\texttt{Gen Int}}\rangle}}\;f=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;f\;()\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\\
\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}{\texttt{Gen Int}}\rangle}}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;0,{\texttt{yield}}\;x\;r\mapsto x+r\;{()}\})\end{array}\end{array}

The explicit boxing and unboxing, as well as the identity modalities,
here are only generated to keep the encoding systematic.
We need not write them in practice as illustrated in

Section

2
.

Our encoding focuses on presenting the core idea and does not consider
advanced features including data types and polymorphism.
In
Section

6
we show how to extend
Met
with these
features and in
Section

C.3
we briefly outline how
to extend the encoding to cover them.

6.
Extensions

In this section we demonstrate that
Met
scales to support data types
and polymorphism including both value and effect polymorphism.
Effect polymorphism helps deal with situations in which it is
useful to refer to one or more effect contexts that differ from the
ambient one (such as the higher-order fork operation
in
Section

2.10
), recovering the full expressive power
of row-based effect systems.
We only discuss the key ideas of extensions here; their full
specification as well as more extensions including shallow
handlers
(
Kammar et al., 2013
;
Hillerström and Lindley, 2018
)
are given in

Appendix

A
.
We prove type soundness and effect safety for all extensions in

Appendix

B
.

6.1.
Making Data Types Crisp

We demonstrate the extensibility of
Met
with data types by extending
it with pair and sum types.
We expect no extra challenge to extend
Met
with algebraic data types.
The syntax and typing rules are shown as follows.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad\Gamma\vdash N:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash(M,N):A\ast B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Pair}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;M:A+B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Inl}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;M:A+B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Inr}}}\par\mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{F}:E\to F\quad\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu_{F}}}}\vdash V:A\ast B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad\quad\Gamma,x\@ifmtarg{\nu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu_{F}}}}}A,y\@ifmtarg{\nu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu_{F}}}}}B\vdash M:A^{\prime}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}{\Gamma\vdash\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{case}}}}{{{\mathbsf{case}}}_{\nu}}\;V\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;(x,y)\mapsto M:A^{\prime}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-CrispPair}}}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\frac{\nu_{F}:E\to The
T-Pair
,
T-Inl
, and
T-Inr
are standard
introduction rules.
The elimination rules
T-CrispPair
and
T-CrispSum
are more
interesting. In addition to normal pattern matching, they interpret
the value
V
under the effect context transformed by certain
modalities
\nu
, which can then be tagged to the variable bindings in
case clauses.
They follow the crisp induction principles of multimodal type
theory
(
Gratzer et al., 2020
;
Shulman, 2018
;
Gratzer et al., 2021
)
.
These crisp elimination rules provide extra expressiveness. For
example, we can write the following function which transforms a sum
of type
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}(A+B)
to another sum of type
({{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A+{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}B)
.
This function is not expressible without crisp elimination rules.

\lambda x^{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}(A+B)}.\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;y=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{\mu}{{{\mathbsf{case}}}}{{{\mathbsf{case}}}_{\mu}}\;y\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;x_{1}\mapsto{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x_{1}),{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;x_{2}\mapsto{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x_{2})\}

6.2.
Polymorphism for Values and Effects

The extensions to syntax and typing rules with polymorphism
are as follows.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Types}&A,B&::=&\cdots\mid\forall\alpha^{K}.A\\
\textrm{Effects}&E&::=&\cdots\mid{\varepsilon\vphantom{\backslash}}\mid{E\backslash L}\\
\textrm{Kinds}&K&::=&\cdots\mid\mathsf{Effect}\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Contexts}&\Gamma&::=&\cdots\mid\Gamma,\alpha:K\\
\textrm{Terms}&M,N&::=&\cdots\mid\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V\mid M\,A\\
\textrm{Values}&V,W&::=&\cdots\mid\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V\mid V\,A\\
\end{array}

\frac{\Gamma,\alpha:K\vdash V:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}{\Gamma\vdash\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V:\forall\alpha^{K}.A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-TAbs}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:\forall\alpha^{K}.B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad}{\Gamma\vdash M\,A:B[A/\alpha]\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-TApp}}}

It may appear surprising that we treat type application
V\,A
as
values. This is useful in practice to allow instantiation inside
boxes. We also extend the semantics to allow reduction in values.

To support effect polymorphism, we extend the syntax of effect
contexts
E
with effect variables
\varepsilon
and introduce a new kind

\mathsf{Effect}
for them.
As is typical for row polymorphism, we restrict each effect type to
contain at most one effect variable.
We also extend the syntax with effect masking
E\backslash L
, which
means the effect types given by masking
L
from
E
.
The latter is needed to keep the syntax of effect contexts closed
under the masking operation
E-L
; otherwise we cannot define

\varepsilon-L
.
In other words, the syntax of effects is the free algebra generated
from extending
D,E
and masking
E\backslash L
with base elements

\cdot
and
\varepsilon
.

The effect equivalence and subeffecting rules are extended in a
relatively standard way.

\frac{}{E\backslash\cdot\equiv E}\par\frac{}{\cdot\backslash L\equiv\cdot}\par\frac{}{(\ell:P,E)\backslash(\ell,L)\equiv E\backslash L}\par\frac{\ell\notin L}{(\ell:P,E)\backslash L\equiv\ell:P,E\backslash L}\par\frac{}{(\varepsilon\backslash L)\backslash L^{\prime}\equiv\varepsilon\backslash(L,L^{\prime})}\par\frac{}{\varepsilon\backslash L\equiv\varepsilon\backslash L}\par\frac{}{\cdot\leqslant\varepsilon\backslash L}\par\frac{}{\varepsilon\backslash L\leqslant\varepsilon\backslash L}

We do not allow non-trivial equivalence or subtyping between different
effect variables.
We always identify effects up to the equivalence relation. That is, we
can directly treat syntax of effects as the free algebra quotiented by
the equivalence relation
E\equiv F
.
Observe that using the equivalence relation, all open effect types
with effect variable
\varepsilon
can be simplified to an equivalent normal
form
D,\varepsilon\backslash L
.
We assume the operation
E-L
is defined for effects
E
in normal
form and extend it with one case for effect variables as

\varepsilon\backslash L-L^{\prime}=\varepsilon\backslash(L,L^{\prime})
.

7.
Simple Bidirectional Type Checking and Elaboration

In this section we outline the design of
Metl
, a basic surface
language on top of
Met
, which uses a simple bidirectional typing
strategy to infer all boxing and unboxing
(
Pierce and Turner, 2000
;
Dunfield and Krishnaswami, 2022
)
.

The bidirectional typing rules for simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus and
modalities of
Metl
and its type-directed elaboration to
Met
are shown in
Figure

4
.
We show the full typing and elaboration rules and discuss our
implementation in
Appendix

D
.

V^{\prime}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\dashrightarrow V^{\prime}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle\dashrightarrow V^{\prime}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\dashrightarrow V^{\prime}$}}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{B-Annotation}}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\Rightarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu}}}}G\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\ \mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle\dashrightarrow M^{\prime}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\dashrightarrow M^{\prime}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle\dashrightarrow M^{\prime}$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\dashrightarrow M^{\prime}$}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash({\overline{\mu}},G)\Rightarrow{\overline{\nu}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\Leftarrow}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\nu}}}}G\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\ \mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle\dashrightarrow\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\mu}}\;x=M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\nu}}\;x$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\dashrightarrow\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\mu}}\;x=M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\nu}}\;x$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle\dashrightarrow\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\mu}}\;x=M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\nu}}\;x$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\dashrightarrow\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\mu}}\;x=M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\nu}}\;x$}}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{B-Switch}}}

Figure 4
.
Representative bidirectional typing and elaboration rules for
Metl
.

As with usual bidirectional typing, we have inference mode

\Gamma\vdash M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\Rightarrow}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
and checking mode

\Gamma\vdash M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\Leftarrow}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
.
They both additionally take the effect context
E
as an input.
The highlighted part
\textstyle\dashrightarrow N
gives the elaborated term
N
in

Met
.

We write
G
for guarded types which do not have top-level modalities,
and
\overline{\mu}
for a sequence of modalities which can be empty.
As a result, every type is in form
\overline{\mu}G
.
We use the following syntactic sugar for boxing and unboxing modality
sequences to simplify the elaboration.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\mu}}\;V&\doteq&\overline{{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}}\;V\\
\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\mu}}\;x=M\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N&\doteq&(\lambda x.\overline{\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;}N)\;M\\
\end{array}

Our bidirectional typing rules are mostly simple and standard.
The most novel part is the usage of an auxiliary function

\mathsf{across}
in
B-Var
defined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{across}(\Gamma,A,\nu,F)&=&\begin{cases}A,&\text{if }\Gamma\vdash A:\mathsf{Abs}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\zeta}}}G,&\text{otherwise, where }A={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu}}}}{G}\text{ and }\nu_{F}\backslash\overline{\mu}_{F}=\zeta_{E}\end{cases}\end{array}

When
A
is absolute, we can always access the variable.
Otherwise, in order to know how far we should unbox the modalities

\overline{\mu}
of the variable, we define a right residual operation

\nu_{F}\backslash\mu_{F}
for the modality transformation relation.
Given
\mu_{F}:E\to F
and
\nu_{F}:F^{\prime}\to F
, the partial operation

\nu_{F}\backslash\mu_{F}
fails if there does not exist
\zeta_{F^{\prime}}

such that
\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\nu_{F}\circ\zeta_{F^{\prime}}
. Otherwise, it gives an
indexed modality such that
\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\nu_{F}\circ(\nu_{F}\backslash\mu_{F})
and for any
\zeta_{F^{\prime}}
with
\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\nu_{F}\circ\zeta_{F^{\prime}}
, we have
\nu_{F}\backslash\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\zeta_{F^{\prime}}
.
Intuitively,
\nu_{F}\backslash\mu_{F}
gives the best solution

\zeta_{F^{\prime}}
for the transformation
\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\nu_{F}\circ\zeta_{F^{\prime}}

to hold.
The concrete definition of
\nu_{F}\backslash\mu_{F}
is given in

Section

D.1
.

B-App
unboxes
M
when it has top-level modalities and inserts
explicit unboxing in the elaborated term.

B-Mod
introduces a lock into the context and inserts
explicit boxing in the elaborated term.

B-Annotation
is standard for bidirectional typing.

B-Switch
not only switches the direction from checking to
inference, but also transforms the top-level modalities when there is
a mismatch by inserting unboxing and re-boxing.
It uses the judgement
\Gamma\vdash(\mu,A)\Rightarrow\nu\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
defined in

Section

4.4
.

Though incorporating polymorphic type inference is beyond the scope of
this paper, we are confident that modal effect types are compatible
with it.
The key observation here is that in the presence of polymorphism, the
problem of automatically boxing and unboxing is closely related to
that of inferring first-class polymorphism.
Modality introduction is analogous to type abstraction (which type
inference algorithms realise through generalisation).
Modality elimination is analogous to type application (which type
inference algorithms realise through instantiation).
As such, one can adapt any of the myriad techniques for combining
first-class polymorphism with Hindley-Milner type inference.
As we adopt a bidirectional type system, we could simply follow the
literature on extending bidirectional typing with sound and complete
inference for higher-rank polymorphism
(
Dunfield and Krishnaswami, 2013
)
,
first-class polymorphism
(
Zhao and d. S. Oliveira, 2022
)
and bounded
quantification
(
Cui et al., 2023
)
.

In the future, we plan to further explore type inference for modal
effect types and in particular design an extension to
OCaml
, building
on and complementing recent work on modal types for

OCaml

(
Lorenzen et al., 2024
)
and making use of existing techniques
for supporting first-class polymorphism.

8.
Related and Future Work

8.1.
Capability-based Effect Systems

Capability-based effect systems such as

Effekt

(
Brachthäuser et al., 2020
;
Brachthäuser et al., 2022
)
and

\textsf{CC}_{<:\Box}

(
Boruch-Gruszecki et al., 2023
)
interpret effects as
capabilities and offer a form of effect polymorphism through
capability passing.

For instance, in
Effekt
the
asList
handler in

Section

2.3
has the following type:

⬇

def

asList
{

f
:

1

\Rightarrow

List
[
Int
]

/

{

Gen
[
Int
]

}

}:

List
[
Int
]

/

{}

The special
block parameter
(or
capability
)

f
can use the effect
Gen
[
Int
]
in addition to
those from the context. The annotation
{

Gen
[
Int
]

}
is
similar to our relative modalities.

A key difference between
Effekt
and
Met
is that
Effekt
requires
blocks to be second-class, whereas
Met
supports first-class functions
by default.
For instance, consider the standard composition function:

compose

f

g

x

=

g

(
f

x
)
. We cannot write this function
naively in
Effekt
as it relies on first-class functions.
One solution is to uncurry it.

⬇

def

composeUncurried
[
A
,

B
,

C
](
x
:

A
){

f
:

A

\Rightarrow

B

}{

g
:

B

\Rightarrow

C

}:

C

/

{}

Note that we move
x
to be the first argument, as
Effekt
requires value parameters to appear before block parameters. We cannot
partially apply
composeUncurried
.

Brachthäuser et al. (2022)
recover first-class functions in
Effekt
by
boxing blocks.
However, such boxed blocks can only use those capabilities specified
in the box types, similarly to our absolute modalities.
With boxes, we can write the curried
compose
with the
following type signature

⬇

def

compose
[
A
,

B
,

C
]{

f
:

A

\Rightarrow

B

}{

g
:

B

\Rightarrow

C

}:

A

\Rightarrow

C

at

{
f
,

g
}

/

{}

which returns a value of type
A

\Rightarrow

C

at

{
f
,

g
}
— a
first-class boxed block.
The annotation
{
f
,

g
}
indicates that this block captures
the capabilities
f
and
g
.
This kind of annotation is reminiscent of effect variables, and indeed
such examples illustrate why
Met
without effect polymorphism is not
expressive enough to encode all of
Effekt
.
If we extend
Met
with effect variables (
Section

6.2
)
then it is possible to encode capability variables like
f

and
g
.

Another key difference between
Met
and
Effekt
is that
Effekt
uses named handlers
(
Biernacki et al., 2020
;
Xie et al., 2022
;
Zhang and Myers, 2019
)
, in which
operations are dispatched to a specific named handler, whereas
Met
uses
Plotkin and Pretnar (2013)
-style handlers, in which operations are
dispatched to the first matching handler in the dynamic context.
Named handlers also provide a form of effect generativity. In future
it would be interesting to explore variants of modal effect types with
named handlers and generative effects
(
de Vilhena and Pottier, 2023
)
.

\textsf{CC}_{<:\Box}

(
Boruch-Gruszecki et al., 2023
)
, the basis for capture
tracking in
Scala
3, also provides succinct types for uncurried
higher-order functions like
composeUncurried
.
As in
Effekt
, the curried version requires the result function to be
explicitly annotated with its capture set
{
f
,
g
}
.
Though
\textsf{CC}_{<:\Box}
is a good fit for
Scala
3, it does rely on
existing advanced features like path-dependent types (especially the
ability of using term variables in types) and implicit parameters.
Modal effect types do not require the language to support such
advanced features.

8.2.
Direct Comparison between
Metl
,
Koka
, and
Effekt

In
Section

2
, we compare the types of
iter
,

asList
,
state
, and
schedule
in

Met
with their counterparts in a row-based effect system similar to

Koka
.
In
Section

8.1
, we discuss the
differences between modal effect types and capability-based effect
systems such as
Effekt
.
Here we present a more direct comparison of the type signatures of
typical programs in
Metl
,
Koka
, and
Effekt
, in order to
demonstrate the practicality of modal effect types.

Invoking Effects

An effect system tracks which effects are invoked.
Consider a function
foo

x

=
do

yield

(
x

+

42)
which uses
the
yield
operation from
Section

2.2
.
Its types in
Metl
,
Koka
, and
Effekt
are as follows.

⬇

foo

:

[
Gen

Int
](
Int

\to

1)

#

METL

foo

:

\forall
<
e
>.

(
x

:

int
)

\to

<
gen
<
int
>|
e
>

()

#

Koka

def

foo
(
x

:

Int
)

:

1

/

{

Gen
[
Int
]

}

#

Effekt

Both
Metl
and
Effekt
support effect subtyping which enables us to
apply
foo
with other effects.

Koka
does not support effect subtyping and uses an effect variable

e
for modularity.

Koka
supports a special typing rule which implicitly inserts effect
variables on function types in covariant position.
Consequently, we can in fact simply write the following type in
Koka
.

⬇

foo

:

(
x

:

int
)

\to

(
gen
<
int
>)

()

#

Koka

Handling Effects

An effect system tracks which effects are handled.
Recall the
asList
handler from
Section

2.3
.
Its types in
Metl
,
Koka
, and
Effekt
are as follows.

⬇

asList

:

[](<
Gen

Int
>(1

\to

1)

\to

List

Int
)

#

METL

:

\forall
<
e
>.

(
action

:

()

\to

<
gen
<
int
>|
e
>

())

\to

e

list
<
int
>

#

Koka

def

asList
{

f
:

1

\Rightarrow

List
[
Int
]

/

{

Gen
[
Int
]

}

}:

List
[
Int
]

/

{}

#

Effekt

Both
Metl
and
Effekt
allow the argument of
asList
to use
the ambient effects in addition to
Gen

Int
.
In
Koka
, we must make the argument polymorphic over other effects.

Functions in Data Types

An effect system should be compatible with algebraic data types.
Consider a pair of
foo
functions as defined in

Section

8.2
.

In
Metl
, we can choose to either share a single absolute
modality between both components, or to have a separate absolute
modality for each component.

⬇

pair1

:

[
Gen

Int
](
Int

\to

1,

Int

\to

1)

pair2

:

([
Gen

Int
](
Int

\to

1),

[
Gen

Int
](
Int

\to

1))

The values
pair1
and
pair2
can be easily
converted between one another.

In
Koka
, we must make both functions effect-polymorphic.
We can choose either to use the same or different effect variables:

⬇

pair3

:

\forall
<
e
,
e1
>.

((
x

:

int
)

\to

<
gen
<
int
>|
e
>

(),

(
y

:

int
)

\to

<
gen
<
int
>|
e1
>

())

pair4

:

\forall
<
e
>.

((
x

:

int
)

\to

<
gen
<
int
>|
e
>

(),

(
y

:

int
)

\to

<
gen
<
int
>|
e
>

())

In
Effekt
, as discussed in

Section

8.1
, we cannot express this
pair of functions directly, as functions are second-class. We must box
the functions to make them first-class.

⬇

pair5

:

Tuple2
[
Int

\Rightarrow

1

/

{

Gen
[
Int
]

}

at

{},

Int

\Rightarrow

1

/

{

Gen
[
Int
]

}

at

{}]

The syntax
at

{}
means that preceding function type is
boxed with no captured capability.

Higher-Order Functions

An effect system should be compatible with higher-order functions.
Consider the standard sequential composition function

compose

f

g

x

=

g

(
f

x
)
.
Its type in
Metl
and
Koka
are as follows.

⬇

compose

:

\forall

a

b

c

.

[]((
a

\to

b
)

\to

(
b

\to

c
)

\to

(
a

\to

c
))

#

METL

compose

:

\forall
<
a
,
b
,
c
,
e
>.

(
f

:

(
a
)

\to
e

b
)

\to

(
g

:

(
b
)

\to
e

c
)

\to

((
x

:

a
)

\to
e

c
)
#

Koka

In
Effekt
, as we discussed in

Section

8.1
, we must either switch to
an uncurried version or box the result with capability variables.

⬇

def

composeUncurried
[
A
,

B
,

C
](
x
:

A
){

f
:

A

\Rightarrow

B

}{

g
:

B

\Rightarrow

}:

C

Effekt

[
A
,

,

C
]{

f
:

A

\Rightarrow

B

}{

g
:

B

\Rightarrow

{
f
,

g
}

/

{}

#

Effekt

In summary, compared to
Koka
,
Metl
provides more succinct
types without effect variables for a rich class of programs.
Compared to
Effekt
,
Metl
supports first-class functions
smoothly with no extra restrictions or capability variables in types.

8.3.
Frank

Our absolute and relative modalities are inspired by
abilities

and
adjustments
in
Frank

(
Lindley et al., 2017
;
Convent et al., 2020
)
.
Absolute modalities and abilities specify the whole effect context
required to run some computation. Relative modalities and adjustments
specify changes to the ambient effect context.
The key difference is that
Frank
is based on a traditional row-based
effect system and implicitly inserts effect variables into
higher-order programs.
This is a fragile syntactic abstraction as discussed in

Section

1
.
In contrast,
Met
exploits modal types to robustly capture the essence
of modular effect programming without effect polymorphism.
As demonstrated in
Section

5
, a core
Frank
-like calculus
with implicit effect variables is expressible in
Met
.

Frank
’s
adaptors
are richer than
Met
’s masking, though we
expect relative modalities to extend readily to encompass the full
power of adaptors.

Unlike adjustments in
Frank
, modal types are first-class types just
like data types and can appear anywhere.
For instance, we can put two functions with modal types in a pair.

⬇

handleTwo

:

[]((<
Gen

Int
>(
1

\to

1
),

<
State

Int
>(
1

\to

1
))

\to

(
List

Int
,

1
))

handleTwo

(
f
,

g
)

=

(
asList

f
,

state

g

42)

8.4.
Relationship Between
Met
and Multimodal Type Theory

The literature on multimodal type theory organises the structure of
modes (objects), modalities (morphisms between objects), and their
transformations (2-cells between morphisms) in a

2-category

(
Gratzer et al., 2020
;
Gratzer, 2023
;
Kavvos and Gratzer, 2023
)

(or, in the case of a single mode, a semiring
(
Abel and Bernardy, 2020
;
Choudhury et al., 2021
;
Orchard et al., 2019
;
Petricek et al., 2014
)
).
In
Met
, modes are effect contexts
E
, modalities are of the form

\mu_{F}:E\to F
, and transformations are of the form
\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\nu_{F}
.
However, 2-categories are insufficient in a system that also includes
submoding.
The extra structure can be captured by moving to
double
categories
, which have an additional kind of vertical morphism
between objects (in
Met
, vertical morphisms are given by the
subeffecting relation
E\leqslant F
), as also proposed
by
Katsumata (2018)
.
Consequently, the transformations do not strictly require the two
modalities to have the same sources and targets, enabling us to have

[]_{F}\Rightarrow[E]_{F}
in
Met
.
The relationship between
Met
and MTT is explained in more detail in

Section

B.1
.

8.5.
Other Related Work

We discuss other related work on effect systems and modal types.

Row-based Effect Systems

Row polymorphism is one popular approach to implementing effect
systems for effect handlers.

Links

(
Hillerström and Lindley, 2016
)
uses Rémy-style row polymorphism with
presence types
(
Rémy, 1994
)
, whereas
Koka

(
Leijen, 2017
)
and

Frank

(
Lindley et al., 2017
)
use scoped rows
(
Leijen, 2005
)
which allow
duplicated labels.

Morris and McKinna (2019)
propose a general framework for comparing different
styles of row types, and
Yoshioka et al. (2024)
propose a similar
framework focusing on comparing effect rows.

Met
adopts Leijen-style scoped rows, but also allows operation labels
to be absent in the spirit of Rémy-style presence types.

Subtyping-based Effect Systems

Eff

(
Bauer and Pretnar, 2013
;
Pretnar, 2014
)
is equipped with an effect system with
both effect variables and sub-effecting based on the type inference
and elaboration described in
Karachalias et al. (2020)
.
The effect system of
Helium

(
Biernacki et al., 2020
)
is based on finite
sets, offering a natural sub-effecting relation corresponding to
set-inclusion. As such, their system aligns closely with

Lucassen and Gifford (1988)
-style effect systems.

Tang et al. (2024)
propose a calculus for effect handlers with effect
polymorphism and sub-effecting via qualified
types
(
Jones, 1994
;
Morris and McKinna, 2019
)
.

Modal Types and Effects.

Choudhury and Krishnaswami (2020)
propose to use the necessity modality to recover
purity from an effectful calculus, which is similar to our empty
absolute modality.

Zyuzin and Nanevski (2021)
extend
contextual modal types
(
Nanevski et al., 2008
)
to algebraic effects and
handlers.
Their system lacks anything like our relative modality and thus cannot
benefit from ambient effect contexts due to strict syntactic
restrictions.
Consequently, they cannot provide concise modular types for
higher-order functions and handlers as
Met
does.

8.6.
Future Work

Future work includes: implementing our system as an extension to

OCaml
; exploring extensions of modal effect types with Fitch-style
unboxing, named handlers, and capabilities; combining modal effect
types with control-flow linearity
(
Tang et al., 2024
)
; and developing a
denotational semantics.

Acknowledgements.

Sam Lindley was supported by UKRI Future Leaders Fellowship “Effect
Handler Oriented Programming” (MR/T043830/1 and MR/Z000351/1).

Data-Availability Statement

The prototype of our surface language
Metl
is
available on Zenodo
(
Tang et al., 2025b
)
.
This prototype implements and extends the bidirectional type checker
outlined in
Sections

7
and
D
.
It type checks all examples in the paper.

Appendix A
Full Specification of
Met
with Extensions

We provide the full specification of
Met
including all
extensions.

A.1.
More Extensions

We first present three more extensions of modal effect types: richer
forms of handlers, boxing pure computations, and commuting
modalities with type abstraction.
We discuss the key ideas of these extensions below and show their full
specification in the following sub-sections.

Absolute and Shallow Handlers

Up to now we have considered only
deep
handlers of the form

{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H
where
M
depends on the ambient effect contexts.
Deep handlers automatically wrap the handler around the body of the
continuation
r
captured in a handler clause, and thus
r
depends on
the ambient effect context.
Though this usually suffices in practice, in some cases we may want
the computation
M
or the continuation to be absolute, i.e.,
independent from the ambient effect context.
We call such handlers
absolute
handlers.
This situation is more prevalent with effect polymorphism.

To support absolute handlers, we extend the handler syntax and typing
rules as follows.

{\frac{{D=\{\ell_{i}:A_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}\}_{i}}\quad\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[D+E]_{F}}}}\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+E}}\quad\quad\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}},x:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[D+E]}}}A\vdash N:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad[\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}},p_{i}:A_{i},r_{i}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]}}}(B_{i}\to B)\vdash N_{i}:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}]_{i}\quad[E]_{F}\Rightarrow{\langle\rangle}_{F}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\spadesuit}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i}:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Handler${}^{\spadesuit}$}}}}

The
T-Handler
♠
rule extends the context with an absolute
lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[D+E]_{F}}}}
specifying the effect context for
M
,
and boxes the continuation
r
with the absolute modality
[E]
,
where
E
exactly gives the effect context after handling.
We put the lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}}
in handler clauses as deep
handlers capture themselves into continuations.
We also extend the handler syntax with
shallow
handlers

{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\dagger}\,M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H
, in which the handler is not automatically
wrapped around the body of continuations, and
absolute shallow

handlers

{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\spadesuit\dagger}\,M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H

(
Kammar et al., 2013
;
Hillerström and Lindley, 2018
)
.

Boxing Computations under Empty Effect Contexts

We have restricted boxes to values in order to guarantee effect
safety.
This restriction is not essential for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}
.
For example, suppose we have

f\@ifmtarg{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]_{F}}}}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]_{F}}}}}}}}(A\to B)
and

x\@ifmtarg{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]_{F}}}}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]_{F}}}}}}}}A
,
it is sound to treat
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\,(f\,x)
as a computation which
returns a value of type
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}B
.
As
f\,x
is evaluated under the empty effect context, we can
guarantee that it cannot get stuck on unhandled operations.

We extend the introduction rule for the empty absolute modality to
allow non-value terms with the following typing rule.

\frac{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]_{F}}}}\vdash M:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\cdot}}}{\Gamma\vdash{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\,M:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-ModAbs}}}

As an example, we can write the following
\mathit{app}
function.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathit{app}&:&\forall\alpha.\forall\beta.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}(\alpha\to\beta)\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\alpha\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\beta\\
\mathit{app}&=&\Lambda\alpha.\Lambda\beta.\lambda f.\lambda x.\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\;f=f\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\,(f\,x)\\
\end{array}

The formula corresponding to the type of this function is commonly
referred to as Axiom K in modal logic and is also satisfied by other
similar modalities such as the safety modality of
Choudhury and Krishnaswami (2020)
.

Commuting Modalities and Type Abstraction

Crisp elimination rules in
Section

6.1
allow us to
commute modalities and data types.
Similarly, it is also sound and useful to commute type abstractions
and modalities.
However, the current modality elimination rule cannot do so, for a
similar reason to why it is not possible to transform

\forall\alpha.A+B
to
(\forall\alpha.A)+(\forall\alpha.B)
in
System F.
We extend modality elimination to the form

\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;\Lambda\overline{\alpha^{K}}x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M
which allows
V
to
use additional type variables in
\overline{\alpha^{K}}
which are abstracted
when bound to
x
.
The extended typing and reduction rules are as follows.

\frac{\nu_{F}:E\to F\quad\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu_{F}}}},\overline{\alpha:K}\vdash V:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad\Gamma,x\@ifmtarg{\nu_{F}\circ\mu_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu_{F}\circ\mu_{E}}}}}\forall\overline{\alpha^{K}}.A\vdash M:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}{\Gamma\vdash\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;\Lambda\overline{\alpha^{K}}.x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{T-Letmod'}}}

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}@{}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\text{{E-Letmod'}}&\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;\Lambda\overline{\alpha^{K}}.x={{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,U\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M&\leadsto&M[(\Lambda\overline{\alpha^{K}}.U)/x]\\
\end{array}

For instance, we can now write a function of type

{\forall\alpha^{K}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}(\forall\alpha.A)

where
\alpha\notin\mathsf{ftv}(\mu)
as follows.

\lambda x^{\forall\alpha^{K}.{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A}.\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;\Lambda\alpha^{K}.y=x\;\alpha\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,y

A.2.

5
gives the syntax of
Met
with all extensions
including data types, polymorphism, and enriched handlers. We highlight
the syntax not present in core
Met
.

A.4.
Auxiliary Operations

Since we extend the syntax of effect contexts
E
, we also need to
define a new case for the operation
E-L
as follows. The
definitions of other operations
D+E
and
L\bowtie D
remain
unchanged from those in
Section

4.2
.
We include the full definition here for easy reference.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}D+E&=&D,E\\
\cdot-L&=&\cdot\\
(\ell:P,E)-L&=&\begin{cases}E-L^{\prime}&\text{if }L\equiv\ell,L^{\prime}\\
\ell:P,(E-L)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}L\bowtie\cdot&=&(L,\cdot)\\
L\bowtie(\ell:P,D)&=&\begin{cases}L^{\prime}\bowtie D&\text{if }L\equiv\ell,L^{\prime}\\
(L^{\prime},(\ell:P,D^{\prime}))&\text{otherwise}\\
\hfil\textstyle\text{where }(L^{\prime},D^{\prime})=L\bowtie D\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

Since we extend the syntax of contexts, we need to extend

\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma)
with one extra trivial case.

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}\mathsf{locks}(\cdot)&=&{{\langle\rangle}}\\
\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}})&=&\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma)\circ\mu_{F}\\
\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma,x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{F}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}}A)&=&\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma)\\
\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma,\alpha:K)&=&\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma)\\
\end{array}

\frac{\Gamma\ni\alpha:K}{\Gamma\vdash\alpha:K}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash A:\mathsf{Abs}}{\Gamma\vdash A:\mathsf{Any}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash[E]\quad\Gamma\vdash A:\mathsf{Any}\quad}{\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]}}}A:\mathsf{Abs}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}\quad\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad}{\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}}}A:K}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash A:\mathsf{Any}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B:\mathsf{Any}}{\Gamma\vdash A\to B:\mathsf{Any}}\par\frac{\Gamma,\alpha:K\vdash A:K^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash\forall\alpha^{K}.A:K^{\prime}}\par\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash 1:\mathsf{Abs}}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B:K\quad}{\Gamma\vdash A\ast B:K}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B:K\quad}{\Gamma\vdash A+B:K}

.
Kinding, well-formedness, and auxiliary rules for
Met
.

Typing rules for

A.6.
Operational Semantics

As type application are treated as values and can reduce, we first
define value normal forms
U
that cannot reduce further as follows.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Value normal forms}&U&::=&x\mid\lambda x^{A}.M\mid\Lambda\alpha^{K}.V\mid{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,U\mid(U_{1},U_{2})\mid{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;U\mid{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;U\\
\end{array}

Consequently, the definition for normal forms in
Section

4.7

is updated as follows.

Definition A.1 (Normal Forms).

We say a term
M
is in a normal form with respect to effect type

E
, if it is either in value normal form
M=U
or of form
M=\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;U]
for
\ell\in E
and
n{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})
.

We also extend the definition of evaluation contexts. The full
definition is given as follows.
Notice that we use value normal forms instead of values.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Evaluation contexts}&\mathcal{E}&::=&[~]\mid\mathcal{E}\;A\mid\mathcal{E}\;N\mid U\;\mathcal{E}\mid{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;\mathcal{E}\mid{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;\mathcal{E}\mid{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\delta}\;\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H\\
&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,\mathcal{E}\mid\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M\mid\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;\Lambda\overline{\alpha^{K}}.x=\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M\\
&&\mid&(\mathcal{E},N)\mid(U,\mathcal{E})\mid\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{case}}}}{{{\mathbsf{case}}}_{\nu}}\;\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;(x,y)\mapsto M\\
&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;\mathcal{E}\mid{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;\mathcal{E}\mid\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{case}}}}{{{\mathbsf{case}}}_{\nu}}\;\mathcal{E}\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;x\mapsto M,{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;y\mapsto N\}\\
\end{array}

Figure

9
shows the operational semantics of
Met
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}@{}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\text{{E-App}}&(\lambda x^{A}.M)\,U&\leadsto&M[U/x]\\
\text{{E-TApp}}&(\Lambda\alpha.V)\,A&\leadsto&V[A/\alpha]\\
\text{{E-Letmod}}&\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x={{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,U\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M&\leadsto&M[U/x]\\
\text{{E-Letmod'}}&\@ifmtarg{\nu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\nu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;\Lambda\overline{\alpha^{K}}.x={{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,U\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M&\leadsto&M[(\Lambda\overline{\alpha^{K}}.U)/x]\\
\text{{E-Mask}}&{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\,U&\leadsto&{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}}\,U\\
{\text{{E-Pair}}}&{{{\mathbsf{case}}}_{\mu}\;(U_{1},U_{2})\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;(x,y)\mapsto N}&{\leadsto}&{N[U_{1}/x,U_{2}/y]}\\
{\text{{E-Inl}}}&{{{\mathbsf{case}}}_{\mu}\;{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;U\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;x\mapsto N_{1},\cdots\}}&{\leadsto}&{N_{1}[U/x]}\\
{\text{{E-Inr}}}&{{{\mathbsf{case}}}_{\mu}\;{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;U\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;y\mapsto N_{2},\cdots\}}&{\leadsto}&{N_{2}[U/y]}\\
\text{{E-Ret}}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;U\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}\,U)/x],\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{where }({{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Op}}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;U]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[U/p,(\lambda y.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}[y]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H)/r],\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{ where }0{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})\text{ and }(\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Ret}}^{\spadesuit}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;U\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[D+E]}\,U)/x]\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{ where }({{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Op}}^{\spadesuit}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\spadesuit}\;\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;U]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&\\
&\hfil\hfil\hfil~N[U/p,({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[E]}\,(\lambda y.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\spadesuit}\;\mathcal{E}[y]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H))/r]\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{ where }0{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})\text{ and }(\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Ret}}^{\dagger}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\dagger}\;U\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}}\,U)/x]\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{ where }({{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Op}}^{\dagger}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\dagger}\;\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;U]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[U/p,(\lambda y.\mathcal{E}[y])/r]\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{ where }0{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})\text{ and }(\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Ret}}^{\spadesuit\dagger}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\spadesuit\dagger}\;U\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[D+E]}\,U)/x]\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{ where }({{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Op}}^{\spadesuit\dagger}&{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\spadesuit\dagger}\;\mathcal{E}[{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;U]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H&\leadsto&N[U/p,({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[D+E]}\,(\lambda y.\mathcal{E}[y]))/r]\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{ where }0{-}\mathsf{free}(\ell,\mathcal{E})\text{ and }(\ell\;p\;r\mapsto N)\in H\\
\text{{E-Lift}}&\mathcal{E}[M]&\leadsto&\mathcal{E}[N],\hfill\text{if }M\leadsto N\\
\end{array}
