{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell_{j}\;U
, we have

\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[D+E]_{F}}}}\vdash U:A_{j}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+E}}.

By the fact that
A_{j}
is pure,
Lemma

B.13
, and context equivalence, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{{[E]_{F}}}}\vdash U:A_{j}\;{{@}}\,{{E}}}\,(4)}.

Observe that
B_{j}
being pure allows
y
to be accessed in any
context.
By
(1)
and a straightforward induction on
\mathcal{E}
we have

\Gamma,y:B_{j},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[D+E]_{F}}}}\vdash\mathcal{E}[y]:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+E}}.

By
[E]_{F}\circ[E]_{E}\circ[D+E]_{E}=[D+E]_{F}
and context equivalence, we have

\Gamma,y:B_{j},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{E}}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[D+E]_{E}}}}\vdash\mathcal{E}[y]:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+E}}.

Since
B_{j}
is pure, we can swap
y:B_{j}
with locks and derive

\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{E}}}},y:B_{j},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[D+E]_{E}}}}\vdash\mathcal{E}[y]:A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{D+E}}.

By
T-Handler
♠
, we have

{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{E}}}},y:B_{j}\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\spadesuit}\;\mathcal{E}[y]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}.

Notice that we can put
H
after absolute locks
because all clauses in
H
have an absolute lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{E}}}}
in
their contexts.
Then by
T-Abs
and
T-Mod
we have

{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{{[E]_{F}}}}\vdash{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[E]}\,(\lambda y.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}^{\spadesuit}\;\mathcal{E}[y]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H):{{{[E]}}}(B_{j}\to B)\;{{@}}\,{{E}}}\,(5)}.

By
(3)
,
(4)
,
(5)
, and
Lemma

B.14
.3 we have

\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[E]_{F}}}}\vdash N_{j}[U/p,({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[F]}\,(\lambda y.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}[y]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H))/r]:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}.

Finally, by
{[E]_{F}\Rightarrow{\langle\rangle}_{F}}
and
Lemma

B.11
.3 we have

\Gamma\vdash N_{j}[U/p,({{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[F]}\,(\lambda y.{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;\mathcal{E}[y]\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H))/r]:B\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}.

Case

Shallow handlers. Similar to the cases of deep handlers.

Case

Data types. Nothing more special than the cases we have
already shown. Introduction rules follows from IHs and reapplying the
same typing rules. Elimination rules require to additionally consider
their corresponding reduction rules.

∎

Appendix C
Specification, Proof, and Discussion of the Encoding

In this section, we show the full encoding of
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
into
Met
, prove
its type preservation, and discuss its extensibility.
The definition of
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is in
Section

5.1
and the definition of

Met
is in
Section

4
.

C.1.
Full Encoding

We repeat the encoding of types and contexts here for easy reference.

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket 1\rrbracket_{E}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle\rangle}}}}1\\
\llbracket A\to^{F}B\rrbracket_{E}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}}}}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket_{F})\\
\llbracket\forall.A\rrbracket_{E}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{E}&=&\cdot\\
\llbracket\Gamma,x:A\rrbracket_{E}&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E},x\@ifmtarg{\mu_{E}}{:}{:_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{E}}}}}A^{\prime}\text{ for }{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A^{\prime}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}\\
\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{F}}}\rrbracket_{E}&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{F},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{F-E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E-F\rangle}}}}\\
\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{F}}}\rrbracket_{\cdot}&=&\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{F},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\\
\end{array}

Figure

11
shows the translation from
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
terms with their
types and effect contexts to
Met
terms.
We use the following syntactic sugar to simply the encoding.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=M\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N&\doteq&(\lambda x.\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N)\;M\\
\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu;\nu}\;x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M&\doteq&\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=V\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{\mu}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\mu}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\nu}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M\\
\end{array}

We use an auxiliary function
\mathsf{topmod}(-)
to extract the top-level
modality.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{topmod}({{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A)&=&\mu\end{array}

[formula]

\frac{\mu\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E})}{x:A\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,x}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Var}}}\par\frac{M:A\to^{E}B\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow M^{\prime}\quad\quad N:A\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow N^{\prime}\quad x\,\mathsf{fresh}}{M\,N:B\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}\;x=M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x\,N^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-App}}}\par\frac{M:B\,!\,F\ \dashrightarrow M^{\prime}\quad\nu\coloneq{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}\quad\mu\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F})}{\lambda^{F}x^{A}.M:A\to^{F}B\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\nu}\,(\lambda x^{\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F}}.\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Abs}}}\par\frac{V:A\,!\,\cdot\ \dashrightarrow V^{\prime}}{\Lambda.V:\forall.A\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\,V^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-EAbs}}}\par\frac{M:\forall.A\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow M^{\prime}\quad x\,\mathsf{fresh}\quad}{M\,@:A[E/]\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\;x=M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-EApp}}}\par\frac{M:A\,!\,\ell,E\ \dashrightarrow M^{\prime}\quad}{{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;M:B\,!\,\ell,E\ \dashrightarrow{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;M^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Do}}}\par\frac{M:A\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow M^{\prime}\quad\mu_{1}\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E})\quad\mu_{2}\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{L+E})\quad}{{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;M:A\,!\,L+E\ \dashrightarrow\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle};\mu_{1}}\;x={{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu_{2}}\,x}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Mask}}}\par\frac{M:A\,!\,\overline{\ell_{i}},E\ \dashrightarrow M^{\prime}\quad N:B\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow N^{\prime}\quad[N_{i}:B\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow N_{i}^{\prime}]_{i}\quad\quad\mu\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\overline{\ell_{i}},E})\quad\mu^{\prime}\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E})\quad\quad N^{\prime\prime}\coloneq\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle};\mu}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{\mu^{\prime}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}\;x={{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}\,x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N^{\prime}\quad\quad[\mu_{i}\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A_{i}\rrbracket_{\cdot})\qquad N_{i}^{\prime\prime}\coloneq\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu_{i}}\;p_{i}=p_{i}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}\;r_{i}=r_{i}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N_{i}^{\prime}]_{i}\quad\quad H=\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i}\quad H^{\prime}\coloneq\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N^{\prime\prime}\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}_{i}\quad}{{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H:B\,!\,E\ \dashrightarrow{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Handler}}}

Figure 11
.
Encoding of
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
in
Met
.

In the term translation, all terms are translated to boxed terms with
proper modalities consistent with those given by the type translation.
Recall that
Met
uses let-style unboxing; we cannot immediately unbox
values at the place we need.
To get a systematic encoding, we
greedily unbox
top-level
modalities for term variables when they are bound, and rebox them when
they are used.

Greedy unboxing happens for variable bindings such as
\lambda
-abstractions and handlers.
In the
R-Abs
case, we unbox the top-level
modality of variable
x
. Additionally, we box the whole function with the relative modality

{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}
, reflecting the effect context transition.
In the
R-Handler
case, we similarly unbox the bound variables
for return and operation clauses.
In the operation clauses (
N_{i}^{\prime\prime}
), we need only unbox the operation
argument
p_{i}
; the resumption function
r_{i}
is introduced under the
current effect context
E
. In the return clause (
N^{\prime\prime}
), we unbox
x

with
{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle}\circ\mu
and then transform this modality to

\mu^{\prime}
given by
\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E})
in order to match the
effect context
E
.

Similar to the
R-Abs
case, the
R-EAbs
case boxes the
translated value with the empty absolute modality.
Similar to the return clauses of the
R-Handler
case, the

R-Mask
case transforms the modality
{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}\circ\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{2}
in order to
match the current effect context
L+E
.

In
R-Var
, we rebox the variable with the appropriate modality
given by the type translation.

As a result of translating all terms to boxed terms, we must insert
unboxing for elimination rules such as
R-App
and
R-EApp
.
Nothing special happens for the
R-Do
case.

C.2.
Proof of Encoding

We prove the encoding from
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
into
Met
in
Section

5
.

See
5.1

Lemma C.1 (Well-scopedness of Derivation Trees).

If the judgement at the bottom of a derivation tree is well-scoped,
then every judgement in the derivation tree is well-scoped.

Proof.

Assume the contrary.
Let
\Gamma_{1},x:_{\varepsilon}A,\Gamma_{2}\vdash M:B\,!\,E
be the top-most judgement in the derivation tree with

x\in\mathsf{fv}(M)
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{F}}}\in\Gamma_{2}
and
A\neq\forall.A^{\prime}
and
A\neq 1
.
By case analysis on whether
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{F}}}\in\Gamma_{2}
was introduced in the derivation tree.

Case

not introduced in the derivation tree: Then the judgement at the bottom
of the derivation tree must contain both the marker and
x
and is not well-scoped for
x
. Contradiction.

Case

introduced in the derivation tree: since we chose the top-most judgement, the judgement must have
introduced the marker by an application of the
R-EAbs
rule. Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}
be the effect variable introduced at
this judgement. Then
\varepsilon\neq\varepsilon^{\prime}
by the side-condition of the
R-EAbs
rule.
We have that
\varepsilon
is the ambient effect at the
R-Var
rule where
x
is used as a free variable,
since we chose the top-most judgement. By the side-condition of the
R-Var
rule, then
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}

or
A=\forall.A^{\prime}
or
A=1
. Contradiction.

∎

In the special case we consider there are no absent types.
This implies that submoding on effects can only add labels to the end.
Furthermore, all labels are drawn from a global environment and thus have
the same operation arrows. This allows us to freely permute them in the effect row.
In this case, we can strengthen the statement to the following:

Corollary C.2 (Transformation from Index).

If
{\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}(F)\leqslant{\langle{L_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{2}\rangle}(F)
and

L_{1}\leqslant F
and
L_{2}\leqslant F
and
L_{1}\bowtie D_{1}=L_{2}\bowtie D_{2}
,
then
{\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}_{F}\Rightarrow{\langle{L_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{2}\rangle}_{F}
.

Proof.

We show that for all
F^{\prime}
with
F\leqslant F^{\prime}
, we have
{\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}(F^{\prime})\leqslant{\langle{L_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{2}\rangle}(F^{\prime})
.
Since all operations are present in
F
, we have that
F^{\prime}=F+\overline{l}
for some collection of labels

\overline{l}
. Then we use that
L_{1}\leqslant F
:

\displaystyle{\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}(F^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}(F+\overline{l})

\displaystyle=D_{1}+((F+\overline{l})-L_{1})

\displaystyle=D_{1}+((F-L_{1})+\overline{l})

\displaystyle={\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}(F)+\overline{l}

and the same for
{\langle{L_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{2}\rangle}(F^{\prime})
. Since
{\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}(F)\leqslant{\langle{L_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{2}\rangle}(F)

and we can freely permute labels,
we have that
({\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}(F)+\overline{l})\leqslant({\langle{L_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{2}\rangle}(F)+\overline{l})
.
∎

The condition that
L_{1}\bowtie D_{1}=L_{2}\bowtie D_{2}
can be checked easily,
where for the composition of modalities we use the fact that
for
{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}={\langle{L_{1}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{1}\rangle}\circ{\langle{L_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D_{2}\rangle}
,
we have
L\bowtie D=(L_{1},L_{2})\bowtie(D_{1},D_{2})
.

Lemma C.3 (First Modality Transformation).

For all
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
:

\frac{}{({\langle{E_{1}-E_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{2}-E_{1}\rangle}\circ{\langle{E_{2}-E_{3}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{3}-E_{2}\rangle})_{E_{1}}\Leftrightarrow{\langle{E_{1}-E_{3}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{3}-E_{1}\rangle}_{E_{1}}}

Proof.

We can use
Corollary

C.2
since
(E_{1}-E_{3})\leqslant E_{1}

and
(E_{1}-E_{2})+L\leqslant E_{1}
where
(L,D)=(E_{2}-E_{3})\bowtie(E_{2}-E_{1})
.
We have:

\displaystyle{\langle{E_{1}-E_{3}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{3}-E_{1}\rangle}(E_{1})

\displaystyle=(E_{3}-E_{1})+(E_{1}-(E_{1}-E_{3}))

\displaystyle=(E_{3}-E_{1})+(E_{1}\cap E_{3})

\displaystyle=E_{3}

and using this calculation:

\displaystyle{\langle{E_{1}-E_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{2}-E_{1}\rangle}\circ{\langle{E_{2}-E_{3}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{3}-E_{2}\rangle}(E_{1})

\displaystyle={\langle{E_{2}-E_{3}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{3}-E_{2}\rangle}({\langle{E_{1}-E_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{2}-E_{1}\rangle}(E_{1}))

\displaystyle={\langle{E_{2}-E_{3}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E_{3}-E_{2}\rangle}(E_{2})

\displaystyle=E_{3}

∎

Lemma C.4 (Second Modality Transformation).

For all
L,E,F
:

\frac{}{{\langle{L+(E-F)}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}_{L+E}\Rightarrow{\langle{(L+E)-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-(L+E)\rangle}_{L+E}}

Proof.

We can use
Corollary

C.2
since
(L+E)-F\leqslant L+E

and
L+(E-F)\leqslant L+E
. We have:

\displaystyle{\langle{(L+E)-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-(L+E)\rangle}(L+E)

\displaystyle=(F-(L+E))+((L+E)-(L+E-F))

\displaystyle=(F-(L+E))+((L+E)\cap F)

\displaystyle=F

and:

\displaystyle{\langle{L+(E-F)}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}(L+E)

\displaystyle=(F-E)+((L+E)-(L+(E-F)))

\displaystyle=(F-E)+(E-(E-F))

\displaystyle=(F-E)+(E\cap F)

\displaystyle=F

∎

Lemma C.5 (Third Modality Transformation).

For all
\overline{\ell_{i}},E,F
:

\frac{}{({\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle}\circ{\langle{\overline{\ell_{i}},E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-\overline{\ell_{i}},E\rangle})_{E}\Rightarrow{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}_{E}}

Proof.

We can use
Corollary

C.2
since

({\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle}\circ{\langle{\overline{\ell_{i}},E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-\overline{\ell_{i}},E\rangle})={\langle{\overline{\ell_{i}},E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-\overline{\ell_{i}},E\rangle}(\overline{\ell_{i}},E)

and
\overline{\ell_{i}},E-F\leqslant\overline{\ell_{i}},E
and
E-F\leqslant E
. We have
{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}(E)=F
and:

\displaystyle{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle}\circ{\langle{\overline{\ell_{i}},E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-\overline{\ell_{i}},E\rangle}(E)

\displaystyle={\langle{\overline{\ell_{i}},E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-\overline{\ell_{i}},E\rangle}({\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle}(E))

\displaystyle={\langle{\overline{\ell_{i}},E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-\overline{\ell_{i}},E\rangle}(\overline{\ell_{i}},E)

\displaystyle=F

∎

Lemma C.6 (Translating Instantiated Types).

For all
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
types
A
:
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}=\llbracket A[E^{\prime}/]\rrbracket_{E,E^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the type
A
.

Case

A={\texttt{Int}}
. Trivial.

Case

A=\forall.A^{\prime}
. Trivial.

Case

A=A^{\prime}\to^{F}B^{\prime}
. Then:

\displaystyle\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}

\displaystyle={\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}(\llbracket A^{\prime}\rrbracket_{F}\to\llbracket B^{\prime}\rrbracket_{F})

\displaystyle\llbracket A[E^{\prime}/]\rrbracket_{E,E^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={\langle{E,E^{\prime}-F,E^{\prime}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F,E^{\prime}-E,E^{\prime}\rangle}(\llbracket A^{\prime}[E^{\prime}/]\rrbracket_{F,E^{\prime}}\to\llbracket B^{\prime}[E^{\prime}/]\rrbracket_{F,E^{\prime}})

By the induction hypothesis we have:

\displaystyle\llbracket A^{\prime}\rrbracket_{F}

\displaystyle=\llbracket A^{\prime}[E^{\prime}/]\rrbracket_{F,E^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\llbracket B^{\prime}\rrbracket_{F}

\displaystyle=\llbracket B^{\prime}[E^{\prime}/]\rrbracket_{F,E^{\prime}}

Since we can freely permute labels:

\displaystyle{\langle{E,E^{\prime}-F,E^{\prime}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F,E^{\prime}-E,E^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle={\langle{E^{\prime},E-E^{\prime},F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E^{\prime},F-E^{\prime},E\rangle}

\displaystyle={\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}

∎

See
5.2

Proof.

By induction on the typing derivation
\Gamma\vdash M:A\,!\,E
. We prove this for each rule of the translation.
As a visual aid, we repeat each rule where we replace the translation premises by the
Met
judgement implied by
the induction hypothesis and the translation in the conclusion by the
Met
judgement we need to prove.

Case

\frac{}{\llbracket\Gamma_{1},x:A,\Gamma_{2}\rrbracket_{E}\vdash\mathsf{rebox}(x;A;E):\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Var}}}

We use the
\mathsf{rebox}(x;A;E)
function defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{rebox}(x;A;E)&=&\begin{cases}{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}{}}\,x,&\text{if }A={\texttt{Int}}\\
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}}\,x,&\text{if }A=A^{\prime}\to^{F}B^{\prime}\\
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\,x,&\text{if }A=\forall.A^{\prime}\end{cases}\\
\end{array}

This function is exactly equivalent to
{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\,x
where
\mu=\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E})

We use the
T-Mod
rule to introduce the box. By cases on the type
A
:

Case

A={\texttt{Int}}
. We can use the
T-Var
rule since
\cdot\vdash{\texttt{Int}}:\mathsf{Abs}
.

Case

A=\forall.A^{\prime}
. Then
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F}={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\llbracket A^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\cdot}
for all
F
.
By rule
MT-Abs
, the pure modality transforms into any other modality and so we can use the
T-Var
rule.

Case

A=A^{\prime}\to^{F}B^{\prime}
. Since the
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
judgement is well-scoped, we have that
\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma_{2})
is
the composition of transition modalities. Furthermore,
\mathsf{locks}(\Gamma^{\prime})\circ{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}:F\to F^{\prime}
for the
context
F^{\prime}
where
x
as introduced and
x
is annotated by the modality
{\langle{F^{\prime}-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-F^{\prime}\rangle}_{F^{\prime}}:F\to F^{\prime}
.
By
Lemma

C.3
, we can use the
T-Var
rule.

Case

\frac{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash M^{\prime}:\llbracket A\to^{E}B\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad\quad\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash N^{\prime}:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad x\,\mathsf{fresh}}{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}{}}\;x=M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x\,N^{\prime}:\llbracket B\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-App}}}

We have
\llbracket A\to^{E}B\rrbracket_{E}={\langle\rangle}{}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket_{E})
. The claim follows
by the
T-Letmod
and
T-App
rules.

Case

\frac{\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{E}}},x:A\rrbracket_{F}\vdash M^{\prime}:\llbracket B\rrbracket_{F}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{F}}\quad\nu\coloneq{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}\quad\mu\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F})}{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\nu}\,(\lambda x^{\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F}}.\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M^{\prime}):\llbracket A\to^{F}B\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Abs}}}

We have
\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{E}}},x:A\rrbracket_{F}=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}}}},x:_{\mu_{F}}A^{\prime}

where
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A^{\prime}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F}
. Further
\llbracket A\to^{F}B\rrbracket_{E}={\langle{E-F}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}F-E\rangle}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{F}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket_{F})
.
The claim follows from the
T-Letmod
,
T-Abs
and
T-Mod
rules.

Case

\frac{\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{E}}}\rrbracket_{\cdot}\vdash V^{\prime}:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\cdot}}}{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\,V^{\prime}:\llbracket\forall.A\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-EAbs}}}

We have
\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit^{\Lambda}_{E}}}\rrbracket_{\cdot}=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}
.
Further,
\llbracket\forall.A\rrbracket_{E}={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}
.
The claim follows from the
T-Mod
rule.

Case

\frac{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash M^{\prime}:\llbracket\forall.A\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad x\,\mathsf{fresh}\quad}{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{[]}\;x=M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;x:\llbracket A[E/]\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-EApp}}}

We have
\llbracket\forall.A\rrbracket_{E}={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{[]}}}\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}
.
By
Lemma

C.6
,
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}=\llbracket A[E/]\rrbracket_{E}
.
The claim follows by the
T-Letmod
rule.

Case

\frac{\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B\in\Sigma\quad\quad\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\ell,E}\vdash M^{\prime}:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\ell,E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\ell,E}}}{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\ell,E}\vdash{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;M^{\prime}:\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\ell,E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\ell,E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Do}}}

Because we only allow pure values in the operation arrows of
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
, we have
that
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\ell,E}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}

and
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\ell,E}=\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\cdot}
,
where
\ell:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}\twoheadrightarrow\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\cdot}
in
Met
.
The claim follows directly by the
T-Do
rule.

Case

\frac{\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{L+E}}}\rrbracket_{E}\vdash M^{\prime}:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad\quad\mu_{1}\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E})\quad\mu_{2}\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{L+E})\quad}{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{L+E}\vdash\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle};\mu_{1}}\;x={{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu_{2}}\,x:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{L+E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{L+E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Mask}}}

We have
\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{L+E}}}\rrbracket_{E}=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{L+E},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{(L+E)-E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E-(L+E)\rangle}}}}
.
By permuting labels, we have

{\langle{(L+E)-E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}E-(L+E)\rangle}={\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}
. The goal follows by the

T-Letmod
,
T-Mask
and
T-Mod
rules if we can show that
x
can be used under the box.
This is clear for integers, since they are pure and otherwise we need to show that

({\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}\circ\mu_{1})_{L+E}\Rightarrow(\mu_{2})_{L+E}
. For
A=\forall.A^{\prime}
this is clear since
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=[]

and
{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\!}\rangle}\circ[]=[]
. For functions, this follows from
Lemma

C.4
.

Case

\frac{\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{E}}}\rrbracket_{\overline{\ell_{i}},E}\vdash M^{\prime}:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\overline{\ell_{i}},E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\ell_{i}},E}}\quad\quad\llbracket\Gamma,x:A\rrbracket_{E}\vdash N^{\prime}:\llbracket B\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\quad[\llbracket\Gamma,p_{i}:A_{i},r_{i}:B_{i}\to^{E}B\rrbracket_{E}\vdash N_{i}^{\prime}:\llbracket B\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}]_{i}\quad\quad\mu\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\overline{\ell_{i}},E})\quad\mu^{\prime}\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E})\quad\quad N^{\prime\prime}\coloneq\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle};\mu}\;x=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{\mu^{\prime}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}{}}\;x={{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}{}}\,x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N^{\prime}\quad\quad[\mu_{i}\coloneq\mathsf{topmod}(\llbracket A_{i}\rrbracket_{\cdot})\qquad N_{i}^{\prime\prime}\coloneq\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\mu_{i}}\;p_{i}=p_{i}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;\@ifmtarg{}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}{{{\mathbsf{let}}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{{\langle\rangle}}\;r_{i}=r_{i}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;N_{i}^{\prime}]_{i}\quad\quad H=\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i}\quad H^{\prime}\coloneq\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto N^{\prime\prime}\}\uplus\{\ell_{i}\;p_{i}\;r_{i}\mapsto N_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i}\quad}{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E}\vdash{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M^{\prime}\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H^{\prime}:\llbracket B\rrbracket_{E}\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}}\quad\textsc{Lab=\text{{R-Handler}}}

We have
\llbracket\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\vardiamondsuit_{E}}}\rrbracket_{\overline{\ell_{i}},E}=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{E},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{{\langle{E-\overline{\ell_{i}},E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}},E-E\rangle}}}}
.
By permuting labels, we have
{\langle{E-\overline{\ell_{i}},E}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}},E-E\rangle}={\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle}
.
In the operation clauses, we have that
\llbracket B_{i}\to^{E}B\rrbracket_{E}={\langle\rangle}{}(\llbracket B_{i}\rrbracket_{E}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket_{E})
.
Because the argument and return of effects are pure, we have that
\llbracket B_{i}\rrbracket_{E}=\llbracket B_{i}\rrbracket_{\cdot}
and

\llbracket A_{i}\rrbracket_{E}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\cdot}
. We need to unbox the argument
p_{i}
and continuation
r_{i}
though.
In the return clause,
Met
gives us
x:{\langle{\!\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\ell_{i}}\rangle}\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\overline{\ell_{i}},E}
, but
we need
x:\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E}
. We achieve this by unboxing
x
fully and then re-boxing it with the
modality
\mu^{\prime}
. This is possible for integers because they are pure, for
\forall
s because
of the
MT-Abs
rule and for functions due to the modality transformation in
Lemma

C.5
.

∎

C.3.
Extensibility of the Encoding

Our encoding in
Section

5
does not consider any extensions
of
Met
.
With the extension of effect polymorphism, the encoding certainly
becomes trivial.
Thus, we only consider extensions of data types and polymorphism for value types
and discuss how to extend the encoding.

Recall that in
Section

5.2
we always translate types to
modal types and perform greedy unboxing and lazy boxing for variables.
For variables of data types such as a pair
(A,B)
, we just need to
further destruct the pair before greedy unboxing, and reconstruct the
pair when lazy boxing.
This is because the translation on its components might give terms of
type
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A^{\prime}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\nu}}}B^{\prime}
with different
modalities which require separate unboxing.
For variables of recursive data types, we need to destruct
only to the extent that the data type is unfolded in the function body
(where we may treat recursive invocations as opaque). While this
requires a somewhat global translation, it does not require
destructing and unboxing the recursive data type more than a small
number of times.

The essential reason for the translation being global comes from the
fact that we use let-style unboxing following MTT.
For modalities with certain structure (right adjoints), it is possible
to use Fitch-style unboxing
(
Clouston, 2018
)
which allows terms to
be directly unboxed without binding
(
Gratzer, 2023
;
Shulman, 2023
)
.
We are interested in exploring whether we could extend
Met
to use
Fitch-style unboxing and thus give a compositional local encoding for
recursive data types.
Fortunately, these issues appear to only cause problems for encoding
but not in practice.
Functional programs typically use pattern-matching in a structured way
that plays nicely with automatic unboxing of bidirectional typing.

Extending the encoding with polymorphism for value types is tricky as the source
calculus
\mathrm{F}^{1}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is not stable under value type substitution.
For instance, the following substitution breaks the condition that
function arrows only refer to the lexically closest effect variable:

(\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.\alpha)[(1\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}1)/\alpha]=\forall{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}.1\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}\varepsilon\}}}}1
.
This exemplifies the fragility of the syntactic approach of
Frank
.
It is possible to still define the encoding by forcing the substituted
type to satisfy the lexical restriction.
We leave the full development of such an encoding as future work.

Appendix D
Specification and Implementation of
Metl

In this section, we provide the syntax and typing rules for

Metl
introduced in
Section

7
and the elaboration
from it to
Met
.
We also briefly discuss our prototype implementation.

D.1.
Syntax and Typing Rules

The syntax of
Metl
is shown in
Figure

12
.
We include extensions of data types and polymorphism in

Section

6
.
For the latter we require explicit type application
M\;A
(explicit
type abstraction is optional since the checking mode tells us when to
introduce type abstraction).

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{~}c@{~}l@{}}\textrm{Types}&A,B&::=&A\to B\mid{{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu}}}A}\mid\alpha\mid A\ast B\mid A+B\\
\textrm{Guarded Types}&G&::=&A\to B\mid\alpha\mid A\ast B\mid A+B\\
\textrm{Masks}&L&::=&\cdot\mid\ell,L\\
\textrm{Extensions}&D&::=&\cdot\mid\ell:P,D\\
\textrm{Effect Contexts}&E,F&::=&\cdot\mid\ell:P,E\\
\textrm{Modalities}&\mu&::=&[E]\mid{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}\\
\textrm{Kinds}&K&::=&\mathsf{Abs}\mid\mathsf{Any}\mid\mathsf{Effect}\\
\textrm{Contexts}&\Gamma&::=&\cdot\mid\Gamma,x:A\mid\Gamma,\alpha:K\mid{\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\mu_{F}}}}}\\
\textrm{Terms}&M,N&::=&x\mid\lambda x.M\mid M\;N\mid{M:A}\mid M\;A\mid{{{\mathbsf{do}}}\;\ell\;M}\\
&&\mid&{{{\mathbsf{mask}}}_{L}\;M}\mid{{\mathbsf{handle}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{with}}}\;H\\
&&\mid&(M,N)\mid{{\mathbsf{case}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;(x,y)\mapsto N\\
&&\mid&{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;M\mid{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;M\mid{{\mathbsf{case}}}\;M\;{{\mathbsf{of}}}\;\{{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;x\mapsto M_{1},{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;y\mapsto M_{2}\}\\
\textrm{Values}&V,W&::=&x\mid\lambda x.M\mid{V:A}\mid V\;A\mid(V,W)\mid{{\mathbsf{inl}}}\;V\mid{{\mathbsf{inr}}}\;V\\
\textrm{Handlers}&H&::=&\{{{\mathbsf{return}}}\;x\mapsto M\}\mid\{(\ell:A\twoheadrightarrow B)\;p\;r\mapsto M\}\uplus H\end{array}

Figure 12
.
Syntax of
Metl
.

The bidirectional typing rules for
Metl
are shown
in
Figure

13
.

We repeat the definition of
\mathsf{across}
used in
B-Var
here
for easy reference.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{across}(\Gamma,A,\nu,F)&=&\begin{cases}A,&\text{if }\Gamma\vdash A:\mathsf{Abs}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\zeta}}}G,&\text{otherwise, where }A={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu}}}}{G}\text{ and }\nu_{F}\backslash\overline{\mu}_{F}=\zeta_{E}\end{cases}\end{array}

For
\mu_{F}:E\to F
and
\nu_{F}:F^{\prime}\to F
, the right residual

\nu_{F}\backslash\mu_{F}
is a partial operation defined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}lcl}{\nu_{F}}&\backslash&{[E]_{F}}&=&[E]_{F^{\prime}}\\
{{\langle{L^{\prime}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D^{\prime}\rangle}_{F}}&\backslash&{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}_{F}}&=&\begin{cases}{\langle{\lfloor D^{\prime}\rfloor+(L-L^{\prime})}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D+F|_{L^{\prime}-L}\rangle}_{D^{\prime}+(F-L^{\prime})},&\text{if }\mathsf{present}(F|_{L^{\prime}-L})\\
\text{fail},&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
{[E]_{F}}&\backslash&{{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}_{F}}&=&\text{fail}\end{array}

We define
\lfloor D\rfloor
as converting an extension to a mask by taking
the multiset of all its labels.
We define
E|_{L}
as the extension derived by extracting the entries in

E
with labels in
L
from the left.
The predicate
\mathsf{present}(D)
checks if all labels in
D
are present.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lfloor\cdot\rfloor&=&\cdot\\
\lfloor\ell:P,D\rfloor&=&\ell,\lfloor D\rfloor\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(\ell:P,E)|_{\ell,L}&=&\ell:P,E|_{L}\\
(\ell^{\prime}:P,E)|_{\ell,L}&=&E|_{\ell,L}\\
\varepsilon|_{\ell,L}&=&\text{fail}\end{array}

B-Mod
introduces a lock and
B-Forall
introduces a type
variable into the context, respectively.

B-Annotation
is standard for bidirectional typing.

B-Switch
not only switches the direction from checking to
inference, but also transforms the top-level modalities when there is
a mismatch.

B-Abs
is standard. Both
B-App
and
B-TApp
unbox
the eliminated term
M
when it has top-level modalities.

B-Do
is standard. For masks and handlers, we have typing rules
in both checking and inference modes.
For
B-HandlerInfer
, we use a partial join operation
A\vee_{\Gamma,F}B
to join the potentially different types of different branches.
The join operation fails when
A
and
B
are different types modulo
top-level modalities; otherwise, it tries to transform the top-level
modalities of
A
and
B
to the same one.
As a special case, if
A
and
B
give some absolute guarded type
G

after removing top-level modalities, the join operation succeeds and
directly returns
G
, which is a general enough solution because an
absolute type has no restriction on its accessibility.

We define join on types as follows.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}lcl}{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu}}}}G&\vee_{\Gamma,F}&{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\nu}}}}G&=&\begin{cases}G,&\text{if }\Gamma\vdash G:\mathsf{Abs}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{({\overline{\mu}}\vee_{F}{\overline{\nu}})}}}G,&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

In order to define
\mu\vee_{F}\nu
, we first define some auxiliary join operations.

We define the join of presence types
P\vee P^{\prime}
as follows, in
order to obtain the minimal type
P^{\prime\prime}
such that
P\leqslant P^{\prime\prime}

and
P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime\prime}
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}lcl}-&\vee&P&=&P\\
P&\vee&-&=&P\\
P&\vee&P^{\prime}&=&\begin{cases}P,&\text{if }P\equiv P^{\prime}\\
\text{fail},&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}

We define the join of effect contexts
E\vee E^{\prime}
as follows, in order
to obtain the minimal effect context
F
such that
E\leqslant F
and

E^{\prime}\leqslant F
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}lcl}\cdot&\vee&\cdot&=&\cdot\\
\ell:P,E&\vee&\ell:P^{\prime},E^{\prime}&=&\ell:(P\vee P^{\prime}),(E\vee E^{\prime})\\
\end{array}

We define the join of an extension and an effect context
D\vee E
as
follows, creating an extension based on
D
by joining the presence types
with those of corresponding labels in
E
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}lcl}\cdot&\vee&E&=&\cdot\\
\ell:P,D&\vee&\ell:P^{\prime},E^{\prime}&=&\ell:(P\vee P^{\prime}),(D\vee E^{\prime})\\
\end{array}

We define the meet of extensions
D\wedge D^{\prime}
as follows.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}lcl}\cdot&\wedge&D&=&\cdot\\
\ell:P,D&\wedge&\ell:P^{\prime},D^{\prime}&=&\ell:(P\vee P^{\prime}),(D\wedge D^{\prime})\\
\ell:P,D&\wedge&D^{\prime}&=&D\wedge D^{\prime},\quad\text{where }\ell\notin\mathsf{dom}(D^{\prime})\\
\end{array}

Note that we take the join of the presence types when the labels are
present in both extensions.

We write
L\cup L^{\prime}
and
L\cap L^{\prime}
for the standard union and
intersection of multisets.

We define the join of modalities
\mu\vee_{F}\nu
at mode
F
as
follows, where
if the join operation succeeds, we have
\mu_{F}\Rightarrow(\mu\vee_{F}\nu)_{F}

and
\nu_{F}\Rightarrow(\mu\vee_{F}\nu)_{F}
, and for any other
\zeta
such that

\mu_{F}\Rightarrow\zeta_{F}
and
\nu_{F}\Rightarrow\zeta_{F}
, we have
(\mu\vee_{F}\nu)_{F}\Rightarrow\zeta_{F}
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}lcl}[E]&\vee_{F}&[E^{\prime}]&=&[E\vee E^{\prime}]\\
[E]&\vee_{F}&{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}&=&\begin{cases}{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\vee E\rangle},&\text{if }E\leqslant D\vee E+(F-L)\\
\text{fail},&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}&\vee_{F}&{\langle{L^{\prime}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D^{\prime}\rangle}&=&\begin{cases}{\langle{L\cap L^{\prime}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\wedge D^{\prime}\rangle},&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\text{if }{\langle{L}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\rangle}_{F}\Rightarrow{\langle{L\cap L^{\prime}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\wedge D^{\prime}\rangle}_{F}\\
\text{and }{\langle{L^{\prime}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D^{\prime}\rangle}_{F}\Rightarrow{\langle{L\cap L^{\prime}}{\mkern 1.0mu\mid\mkern 1.0mu}D\wedge D^{\prime}\rangle}_{F}\\
\end{array}\\
\text{fail},&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\end{array}

The third case may appear surprising since it takes the meet of
masks and extensions.
It works because the
MT-Shrink
rule in
Section

4.3

allows us to remove corresponding elements from masks and extensions
simultaneously.
The side conditions guarantee that both modalities can be transformed
to their join.

The introduction rules of data types are standard.
For their elimination, we have both checking and inference version.
Both
B-CrispPair
and
B-CrispSum
extract the top-level
modalities of the data values and distribute them to the types of the
variables for their components.
We also use the join operation to unify the different branches of

B-CrispSumInfer
.

Figure 13
.
Bidirectional typing rules for
Metl
.

D.2.
Elaboration and Implementation

It is easy to elaborate
Metl
terms into
Met
terms in a
type-directed manner.
We formally define the elaboration alongside the typing rules in

Figures

14
and
15
.
We have
\Gamma\vdash M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\Rightarrow}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\ \mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle\dashrightarrow N$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\dashrightarrow N$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle\dashrightarrow N$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\dashrightarrow N$}}}
for the inference mode
and
\Gamma\vdash M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\Leftarrow}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}\ \mathchoice{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\displaystyle\dashrightarrow N$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\textstyle\dashrightarrow N$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptstyle\dashrightarrow N$}}}{{\lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.4}\hbox{\pagecolor{hlcolor}\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}$\scriptscriptstyle\dashrightarrow N$}}}
for the checking mode, both
of which elaborates
M
in
Metl
to
N
in
Met
.

We use the following auxiliary functions in the elaboration.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{unmod}(M;\overline{\mu})&=&(\lambda y.{{\mathbsf{let\;mod}}}_{\overline{\mu}}\;y=y\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;y)\;M\\
\mathsf{unvar}(x;A;M)&=&{{\mathbsf{let\;mod}}}_{\overline{\mu}}\;{x}=x\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;M\quad\text{ where }A={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu}}}}G\\
\\
\mathsf{join}_{\Gamma,E}(M_{1}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu_{1}}}}}G_{1},M_{2}:{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu_{2}}}}}G_{2},\cdots)&=&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}({{\mathbsf{let\;mod}}}_{\overline{\mu_{1}}}\;x=M_{1}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\zeta}}\;x,\\
\hphantom{(}{{\mathbsf{let\;mod}}}_{\overline{\mu_{2}}}\;x=M_{2}\;{{\mathbsf{in}}}\;{{\mathbsf{mod}}}_{\overline{\zeta}}\;x,\cdots)\end{array}\\
\hfil\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\lx@intercol\quad\text{ where }{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu_{1}}}}}G_{1}\vee_{\Gamma,E}{{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\mu_{2}}}}}G_{2}\vee_{\Gamma,E}\cdots={{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{\overline{\zeta}}}}G\\
\end{array}

The core idea of the elaboration is similar to the greedy unboxing
strategy of the encoding we have in
Section

C.1
.
For
B-Abs
(and also handler rules), we immediately fully unbox
the bound variables.
For
B-Mod
, we insert explicit boxing. For
B-Var
, we
re-box them with the appropriate modality
\zeta
.
For
B-Switch
, we insert an unboxing followed by boxing.
For
B-App
and
B-TApp
, we unbox
\overline{\mu}
.
For
B-HandlerCheck
and
B-HandlerInfer
, we unbox the
bound variables in handler clauses. We do not need to unbox the
continuation functions since they have no top-level modality.
For
B-CrispPairCheck/Infer
and
B-CrispSumCheck/Infer
, we
unbox
V
before case splitting.
Also, when the
\wedge_{E}
operation is used (such as in

B-CrispSumInfer
and
B-HandlerInfer
), we use
\mathsf{join}
to
unbox the terms correspondingly.

[formula]

Figure 15
.
Elaboration from
Metl
to
Met
(part II).

We implement a prototype of
Metl
with all features mentioned
above as well as algebraic data types and pattern matching.
We do not encounter any challenges in generalising the pairs and sums
to algebraic data types.
In our implementation, we do not strictly follow the conventional
bidirectional typing approach, which distinguishes between the
checking and inference mode as in the above rules.
Instead, we use the form
\Gamma\vdash M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\Leftarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.1289,0.1797}{{S}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{\Rightarrow}}}A\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{@}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1797,0.4023,0.9727}{E}}
where each rule
has an input shape
S
and output type
A
, similar to contextual
typing
(
Xue and Oliveira, 2024
)
.
A shape
S
is a type with some holes.
When
S
is empty, we are in inference mode; when
S
is a complete
type, we are in checking mode; otherwise, we can still use
S
to pass
in partial type information which could allow us to type check more
programs.

Our implementation supports polymorphism with explicit type
instantiation.
As we have discussed in
Section

7
, it is natural to
extend it with inference for polymorphism, following the literature on
bidirectional typing
(
Dunfield and Krishnaswami, 2013
;
Zhao and d. S. Oliveira, 2022
;
Cui et al., 2023
)
.
We plan to explore this extension in the future.
